Implementations should\n33:  * not use this class directly; they should inherit from {@link org.apache.kafka.connect.source.SourceConnector SourceConnector}\n34:  * or {@link org.apache.kafka.connect.sink.SinkConnector SinkConnector}.\n35:  * </p>\n36:  * <p>\n37:  * Connectors have two primary roles. First, given some configuration, they are responsible for\n38:  * creating configurations for a set of {@link Task}s that split up the data processing. For\n39:  * example, a database Connector might create Tasks by dividing the set of tables evenly among\n40:  * tasks. Second, they are responsible for monitoring inputs for changes that require\n41:  * reconfiguration and notifying the Kafka Connect runtime via the {@link ConnectorContext}.             "excerpt": "1: ### JMH-Benchmarks module\n2: \n3: This module contains benchmarks written using [JMH](https://openjdk.java.net/projects/code-tools/jmh/) from OpenJDK.\n4: Writing correct micro-benchmarks in Java (or another JVM language) is difficult and there are many non-obvious pitfalls (many\n5: due to compiler optimizations). JMH is a framework for running and analyzing benchmarks (micro or macro) written in Java (or\n6: another JVM language).\n7: \n8: ### Running benchmarks\n9: \n10: If you want to set specific JMH flags or only run certain benchmarks, passing arguments via\n11: gradle tasks is cumbersome. These are simplified by the provided `jmh.sh` script.\n12: \n13: The default behavior is to run all benchmarks:\n14: \n15:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh\n16:     \n17: Pass a pattern or name after the command to select the benchmarks:\n18: \n19:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh LRUCacheBenchmark\n20: \n21: Check which benchmarks that match the provided pattern:\n22: \n23:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -l LRUCacheBenchmark\n24: \n25: Run a specific test and override the number of forks, iterations and warm-up iteration to `2`:\n26: \n27:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -f 2 -i 2 -wi 2 LRUCacheBenchmark\n28: \n29: Run a specific test with async and GC profilers on Linux and flame graph output:\n30: \n31:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof gc -prof async:libPath=/path/to/libasyncProfiler.so\\;output=flamegraph LRUCacheBenchmark\n32: \n33: The following sections cover async profiler and GC profilers in more detail.\n34: \n35: ### Using JMH with async profiler\n36: \n37: It's good practice to check profiler output for microbenchmarks in order to verify that they represent the expected\n38: application behavior and measure what you expect to measure. Some example pitfalls include the use of expensive mocks\n39: or accidental inclusion of test setup code in the benchmarked code. JMH includes\n40: [async-profiler](https://github.com/jvm-profiling-tools/async-profiler) integration that makes this easy:\n41: \n42:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof async:libPath=/path/to/libasyncProfiler.so\n43: \n44: With flame graph output (the semicolon is escaped to ensure it is not treated as a command separator):\n45: \n46:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof async:libPath=/path/to/libasyncProfiler.so\\;output=flamegraph\n47: \n48: Simultaneous cpu, allocation and lock profiling with async profiler 2.0 and jfr output (the semicolon is\n49: escaped to ensure it is not treated as a command separator):\n50: \n51:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof async:libPath=/path/to/libasyncProfiler.so\\;output=jfr\\;alloc\\;lock LRUCacheBenchmark\n52: \n53: A number of arguments can be passed to configure async profiler, run the following for a description:\n54: \n55:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof async:help\n56: \n57: ### Using JMH GC profiler\n58: \n59: It's good practice to run your benchmark with `-prof gc` to measure its allocation rate:\n60: \n61:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof gc\n62: \n63: Of particular importance is the `norm` alloc rates, which measure the allocations per operation rather than allocations\n64: per second which can increase when you have make your code faster.\n65: \n66: ### Running JMH outside of gradle\n67: \n68: The JMH benchmarks can be run outside of gradle as you would with any executable jar file:\n69: \n70:     java -jar <kafka-repo-dir>/jmh-benchmarks/build/libs/kafka-jmh-benchmarks-*.jar -f2 LRUCacheBenchmark\n71: \n72: ### Writing benchmarks\n73: \n74: For help in writing correct JMH tests, the best place to start is the [sample code](https://hg.openjdk.java.net/code-tools/jmh/file/tip/jmh-samples/src/main/java/org/openjdk/jmh/samples/) provided\n75: by the JMH project.\n76: \n77: Typically, JMH is expected to run as a separate project in Maven. The jmh-benchmarks module uses\n78: the [gradle shadow jar](https://github.com/johnrengelman/shadow) plugin to emulate this behavior, by creating the required\n79: uber-jar file containing the benchmarking code and required JMH classes.  \n80: ",

            "excerpt": "1: ### JMH-Benchmarks module\n2: \n3: This module contains benchmarks written using [JMH](https://openjdk.java.net/projects/code-tools/jmh/) from OpenJDK.\n4: Writing correct micro-benchmarks in Java (or another JVM language) is difficult and there are many non-obvious pitfalls (many\n5: due to compiler optimizations). JMH is a framework for running and analyzing benchmarks (micro or macro) written in Java (or\n6: another JVM language).\n7: \n8: ### Running benchmarks\n9: \n10: If you want to set specific JMH flags or only run certain benchmarks, passing arguments via\n11: gradle tasks is cumbersome. These are simplified by the provided `jmh.sh` script.\n12: \n13: The default behavior is to run all benchmarks:\n14: \n15:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh\n16:     \n17: Pass a pattern or name after the command to select the benchmarks:\n18: \n19:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh LRUCacheBenchmark\n20: \n21: Check which benchmarks that match the provided pattern:\n22: \n23:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -l LRUCacheBenchmark\n24: \n25: Run a specific test and override the number of forks, iterations and warm-up iteration to `2`:\n26: \n27:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -f 2 -i 2 -wi 2 LRUCacheBenchmark\n28: \n29: Run a specific test with async and GC profilers on Linux and flame graph output:\n30: \n31:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof gc -prof async:libPath=/path/to/libasyncProfiler.so\\;output=flamegraph LRUCacheBenchmark\n32: \n33: The following sections cover async profiler and GC profilers in more detail.\n34: \n35: ### Using JMH with async profiler\n36: \n37: It's good practice to check profiler output for microbenchmarks in order to verify that they represent the expected\n38: application behavior and measure what you expect to measure. Some example pitfalls include the use of expensive mocks\n39: or accidental inclusion of test setup code in the benchmarked code. JMH includes\n40: [async-profiler](https://github.com/jvm-profiling-tools/async-profiler) integration that makes this easy:\n41: \n42:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof async:libPath=/path/to/libasyncProfiler.so\n43: \n44: With flame graph output (the semicolon is escaped to ensure it is not treated as a command separator):\n45: \n46:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof async:libPath=/path/to/libasyncProfiler.so\\;output=flamegraph\n47: \n48: Simultaneous cpu, allocation and lock profiling with async profiler 2.0 and jfr output (the semicolon is\n49: escaped to ensure it is not treated as a command separator):\n50: \n51:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof async:libPath=/path/to/libasyncProfiler.so\\;output=jfr\\;alloc\\;lock LRUCacheBenchmark\n52: \n53: A number of arguments can be passed to configure async profiler, run the following for a description:\n54: \n55:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof async:help\n56: \n57: ### Using JMH GC profiler\n58: \n59: It's good practice to run your benchmark with `-prof gc` to measure its allocation rate:\n60: \n61:     ./jmh-benchmarks/jmh.sh -prof gc\n62: \n63: Of particular importance is the `norm` alloc rates, which measure the allocations per operation rather than allocations\n64: per second which can increase when you have make your code faster.\n65: \n66: ### Running JMH outside of gradle\n67: \n68: The JMH benchmarks can be run outside of gradle as you would with any executable jar file:\n69: \n70:     java -jar <kafka-repo-dir>/jmh-benchmarks/build/libs/kafka-jmh-benchmarks-*.jar -f2 LRUCacheBenchmark\n71: \n72: ### Writing benchmarks\n73: \n74: For help in writing correct JMH tests, the best place to start is the [sample code](https://hg.openjdk.java.net/code-tools/jmh/file/tip/jmh-samples/src/main/java/org/openjdk/jmh/samples/) provided\n75: by the JMH project.\n76: \n77: Typically, JMH is expected to run as a separate project in Maven. The jmh-benchmarks module uses\n78: the [gradle shadow jar](https://github.com/johnrengelman/shadow) plugin to emulate this behavior, by creating the required\n79: uber-jar file containing the benchmarking code and required JMH classes.  \n80: ",

the same partition number from every subscribed topic when co-partitioning is possible.</li>\n43:  * </ol>\n44:  *\n45:  * Co-partitioning is possible when the below conditions are satisfied:\n46:  * <ol>\n47:  *   <li>All the members are subscribed to the same set of topics.</li>\n48:  *   <li>All the topics have the same number of partitions.</li>\n49:  * </ol>\n50:  *\n51:  * Co-partitioning is useful in performing joins on data streams.\n52:  *\n53:  * <p>For example, suppose there are two members M0 and M1, two topics T1 and T2, and each topic has 3 partitions.\n54:  *\n55:  * <p>The co-partitioned assignment will be:\n56:  * <ul>\n57:  * <li><code>   M0: [T1P0, T1P1, T2P0, T2P1]    </code></li>\n58:  * <li><code>   M1: [T1P2, T2P2]                </code></li>\n59:  * </ul>\n60:  *\n61:  * Since the introduction of static membership, we could leverage <code>member.instance.id</code> to make the\n62:  * assignment behavior more sticky.\n63:  * For the above example, after one rolling bounce, the group coordinator will attempt to assign new member Ids towards\n64:  * members, for example if <code>M0</code> -&gt; <code>M3</code> <code>M1</code> -&gt; <code>M2</code>.\n65:  *\n66:  * <p>The assignment could be completely shuffled to:\n67:  * <ul>\n68:  * <li><code>   M3 (was M0): [T1P2, T2P2]               (before it was [T1P0, T1P1, T2P0, T2P1])  </code></li>\n69:  * <li><code>   M2 (was M1): [T1P0, T1P1, T2P0, T2P1]   (before it was [T1P2, T2P2])  </code></li>\n70:  * </ul>\n71:  *\n72:  * The assignment change was caused by the change of <code>member.id</code> relative order, and\n73:  * can be avoided by setting the instance.id.\n74:  * Members will have individual instance Ids <code>I0</code>, <code>I1</code>. Subscription pattern doesn't change for any member.\n78:  *\n79:  * <p>The assignment will always be:\n80:  * <ul>\n81:  * <li><code>   I0: [T1P0, T1P1, T2P0, T2P1]    </code></li>\n82:  * <li><code>   I1: [T1P2, T2P2]                </code></li>\n83:  * </ul>\n84:  * <p>\n85:  */\n86: public class RangeAssignor implements ConsumerGroupPartitionAssignor {\n87:     public static final String NAME = \"range\";\n88: \n89:     @Override\n90:     public String name() {\n91:         return NAME;\n92:     }\n93: \n94:     /**\n95:      * Metadata for a topic including partition and subscription details.\n96:      */\n97:     private static class TopicMetadata {\n98:         private final Uuid topicId;\n99:         private final int numPartitions;\n100:         private int numMembers;",

<ol>\n47:  *   <li>All the members are subscribed to the same set of topics.</li>\n48:  *   <li>All the topics have the same number of partitions.</li>\n49:  * </ol>\n50:  *\n51:  * Co-partitioning is useful in performing joins on data streams.\n52:  *\n53:  * <p>For example, suppose there are two members M0 and M1, two topics T1 and T2, and each topic has 3 partitions.\n54:  *\n55:  * <p>The co-partitioned assignment will be:\n56:  * <ul>\n57:  * <li><code>   M0: [T1P0, T1P1, T2P0, T2P1]    </code></li>\n58:  * <li><code>   M1: [T1P2, T2P2]                </code></li>\n59:  * </ul>\n60:  *\n61:  * Since the introduction of static membership, we could leverage <code>member.instance.id</code> to make the\n62:  * assignment behavior more sticky.\n63:  * For the above example, after one It is free otherwise, and\n33:  * giving it away revokes no partition.\n34:  *\n35:  * <p>The step applies a single rule until it no longer applies. The member with the largest\n36:  * assignment, among those with an extra partition that can move, gives one to the smallest member\n37:  * that can take one of its extra partitions. A member can take an extra partition when it\n38:  * subscribes to the topic, has no extra partition of the topic, and is at least two partitions\n39:  * smaller. On equal assignment sizes, a giver with a free extra partition gives first, and a\n40:  * receiver owning more than the minimum quota of a topic with extra partitions receives first,\n41:  * since only such a receiver can keep one more of its partitions. Member order breaks the\n42:  * remaining ties. The giver gives the extra partition whose move revokes the fewest partitions. It\n43:  * tries its free extra partitions first, then its owned ones, and in both lists prefers a topic\n44:  * that the receiver owns more than the minimum quota of. Ties are broken by taking the last such\n45:  * topic in the list of the giver.\n46:  *\n47:  * <p>Every move goes from a member to one with at least two partitions fewer, so it lowers the sum\n48:  * of the squares of the assignment sizes by at least two, and the step ends. It ends when no extra\n49:  * partition can move to a subscriber with at least two partitions fewer, which is the balance\n50:  * property. With every member subscribed to the same topics, all assignment sizes are then within\n51:  * one of each other, since a member with two partitions more than another has an extra partition\n52:  * of a topic the other does not have. Nothing moves when the assignment already has the property.\n53:  *\n54:  * <p>Pairing the largest member with the smallest one across cohorts lets a member give an extra\n55:  * partition to a member of another cohort that needs one. Balancing each cohort on its own could\n56:  * instead take a move in each of the two cohorts.\n57:  *\n58:  * <p>The members with extra partitions wait in a heap, the largest first. A member without a\n59:  * receiver is parked until a move may give it one, see {@link #wake}. Receiving an extra partition\n60:  * also wakes a member, since it is then larger and has a new topic to give. The step ends when the\n61:  * largest giver is less than two partitions above the smallest member.\n62:  *\n63:  * <p>Receivers are searched cohort by cohort, starting with the cohort of the smallest member, and\n64:  * within a cohort from its smallest member. A cohort is skipped when each of its members already\n65:  * has an extra partition of every topic of the giver that the cohort subscribes to. A member is\n66:  * known to be able to take an extra partition of the giver when the member has fewer extra\n67:  * partitions in all than the giver has of topics of the member's cohort. It is free otherwise, and\n33:  * giving it away revokes no partition.\n34:  *\n35:  * <p>The step applies a single rule until it no longer applies. The member with the largest\n36:  * assignment, among those with an extra partition that can move, gives one to the smallest member\n37:  * that can take one of its extra partitions. A member can take an extra partition when it\n38:  * subscribes to the topic, has no extra partition of the topic, and is at least two partitions\n39:  * smaller. On equal assignment sizes, a giver with a free extra partition gives first, and a\n40:  * receiver owning more than the minimum quota of a topic with extra partitions receives first,\n41:  * since only such a receiver can keep one more of its partitions. Member order breaks the\n42:  * remaining ties. The giver gives the extra partition whose move revokes the fewest partitions. It\n43:  * tries its free extra partitions first, then its owned ones, and in both lists prefers a topic\n44:  * that the receiver owns more than the minimum quota of. Ties are broken by taking the last such\n45:  * topic in the list of the giver.\n46:  *\n47:  * <p>Every move goes from a member to one with at least two partitions fewer, so it lowers the sum\n48:  * of the squares of the assignment sizes by at least two, and the step ends. It ends when no extra\n49:  * partition can move to a subscriber with at least two partitions fewer, which is the balance\n50:  * property. With every member subscribed to the same topics, all assignment sizes are then within\n51:  * one of each other, since a member with two partitions more than another has an extra partition\n52:  * of a topic the other does not have. Nothing moves when the assignment already has the property.\n53:  *\n54:  * <p>Pairing the largest member with the smallest one across cohorts lets a member give an extra\n55:  * partition to a member of another cohort that needs one. Balancing each cohort on its own could\n56:  * instead take a move in each of the two cohorts.\n57:  *\n58:  * <p>The members with extra partitions wait in a heap, the largest first. A member without a\n59:  * receiver is parked until a move may give it one, see {@link #wake}. Receiving an extra partition\n60:  * also wakes a member, since it is then larger and has a new topic to give. The step ends when the\n61:  * largest giver is less than two partitions above the smallest member.\n62:  *\n63:  * <p>Receivers are searched cohort by cohort, starting with the cohort of the smallest member, and\n64:  * within a cohort from its smallest member. A cohort is skipped when each of its members already\n65:  * has an extra partition of every topic of the giver that the cohort subscribes to. A member is\n66:  * known to be able to take an extra partition of the giver when the member has fewer extra\n67:  * partitions in all than the giver has of topics of the member's cohort.
